[Open in Claude](https://claude.ai/new?q=Read%20https%3A%2F%2Fwormhole.com%2Fdocs%2Fdocs%2Fai%2Fpages%2Fprotocol-security.md%20so%20I%20can%20ask%20questions%20about%20it.)\n\n## Core Security Assumptions [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#core-security-assumptions \"Permanent link\")\n\nAt its core, Wormhole is secured by a network of [Guardian](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/guardians/) nodes that validate and sign messages. If a super majority (e.g., 13 out of 19) of Guardians sign the same message, it can be considered valid. A smart contract on the target chain will verify the signatures and format of the message before approving any transaction.\n\n- Wormhole's core security primitive is its signed messages (signed [VAAs](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/vaas/)).\n- The Guardian network is currently secured by a collection of 19 of the world's top [validator companies](https://wormhole-foundation.github.io/wormhole-dashboard/#/?endpoint=Mainnet).\n- Guardians produce signed state attestations (signed VAAs) when requested by a Core Contract integrator.\n- Every Guardian runs full nodes (rather than light nodes) of every blockchain in the Wormhole network, so if a blockchain suffers a consensus attack or hard fork, the blockchain will disconnect from the network rather than potentially produce invalid signed VAAs.\n- Any Signed VAA can be verified as authentic by the Core Contract of any other chain.\n- The [Executor](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/relayers/executor-framework/) is considered untrusted in the Wormhole ecosystem. It can affect message availability (timing of delivery) but cannot alter or forge VAAs, as validity is enforced by Guardian signatures.\n\nIn summary:\n\n- **Core integrators are not exposed to risk from chains and contracts they do not integrate with**.\n- By default, you only trust Wormhole's signing process and the core contracts of the chains you're on.\n- You can expand your contract and chain dependencies as you see fit.\n\nCore assumptions aside, many other factors impact the real-world security of decentralized platforms. Here is more information on additional measures that have been put in place to ensure the security of Wormhole.\n\n## Guardian Network [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#guardian-network \"Permanent link\")\n\nWormhole is an evolving platform. While the canonical Guardian set consists of 19 members, not all chains require all 19 Guardians to perform direct on-chain observation. For delegated chains, a delegated subset of Guardians performs direct on-chain observation while the final VAA signature threshold remains 13-of-19.\n\nDelegated Guardian Sets introduces a per-chain trust assumption: the delegate quorum threshold determines how many Delegated Guardians must independently agree before Canonical Guardians sign. This model does not weaken the final 13-of-19 VAA requirement, but it means the observation layer for that chain depends on fewer nodes. The `WormholeDelegatedGuardians` governance contract allows these thresholds to be adjusted as network requirements evolve.\n\n### Governance [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#governance \"Permanent link\")\n\nGovernance is the process through which contract upgrades happen. Guardians manually vote on governance proposals that originate inside the Guardian Network and are then submitted to ecosystem contracts.\n\nThis means that governance actions are held to the same security standard as the rest of the system. A two-thirds supermajority of the Guardians is required to pass any governance action.\n\nGovernance messages can target any of the various wormhole modules, including the core contracts and all currently deployed Wrapped Token Transfers (WTT) contracts. When a Guardian signs such a message, its signature implies a vote on the action in question. Once more than two-thirds of the Guardians have signed, the message and governance action are considered valid.\n\nAll governance actions and contract upgrades have been managed via Wormhole's on-chain governance system.\n\nVia governance, the Guardians can:\n\n- Change the current Guardian set.\n- Expand the Guardian set.\n- Upgrade ecosystem contract implementations.\n- Configure per-chain Delegated Guardian sets and security thresholds via the `WormholeDelegatedGuardians` contract.\n\nThe governance system is fully open source in the core repository. See the [Open Source section](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/#open-source) for contract source.\n\n## Monitoring [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#monitoring \"Permanent link\")\n\nA key element of Wormhole's defense-in-depth strategy is that each Guardian is a highly competent validator company with its own in-house processes for running, monitoring, and securing blockchain operations. This heterogeneous approach to monitoring increases the likelihood that fraudulent activity is detected and reduces the number of single failure points in the system.\n\nFor full Guardian Set chains, all Guardians run full nodes and independently monitor block production and contract activity. For delegated chains, Delegated Guardians perform direct on-chain monitoring while Canonical Guardians monitor delegate observations and enforce quorum safeguards.\n\nGuardians monitor:\n\n- **Block production and consensus of each blockchain**: If a blockchain's consensus is violated, it will be disconnected from the network until the Guardians resolve the issue.\n- **Smart contract level data**: Via processes like the Governor, Guardians constantly monitor the circulating supply and token movements across all supported blockchains.\n- **Guardian level activity**: The Guardian Network functions as an autonomous decentralized computing network, ensuring independent security measures across its validators.\n\n## Asset Layer Protections [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#asset-layer-protections \"Permanent link\")\n\nOne key strength of the Wormhole ecosystem is the Guardians' ability to validate and protect the integrity of assets across multiple blockchains.\n\nTo enforce the Wormhole Asset Layer's core protections, the Global Accountant tracks the total circulating supply of all Wormhole assets across all chains, preventing any blockchain from bridging assets that could violate the supply invariant.\n\nFor delegated chains, delegate quorum must be reached before Canonical Guardians contribute signatures, ensuring that per-chain delegate thresholds are respected without altering the Global Accountant's 13-of-19 security model.\n\nIn addition to the Global Accountant, Guardians may only sign transfers that do not violate the requirements of the Governor. The [Governor](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/whitepapers/0007_governor.md) tracks inflows and outflows of all blockchains and delays suspicious transfers that may indicate an exploit.\n\n## Open Source [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#open-source \"Permanent link\")\n\nWormhole builds in the open and is always open source.\n\n- **[Wormhole core repository](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole)**\n- **[Wormhole Foundation GitHub organization](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation)**\n- **[Wormhole contract deployments](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/core-contracts/)**\n\n## Audits [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#audits \"Permanent link\")\n\nWormhole has been heavily audited, with _29 third-party audits completed_ and more started. Audits have been performed by the following firms:\n\n- [Trail of Bits](https://www.trailofbits.com/)\n- [Neodyme](https://neodyme.io/en/)\n- [Kudelski](https://kudelskisecurity.com/)\n- [OtterSec](https://osec.io/)\n- [Certik](https://www.certik.com/)\n- [Hacken](https://hacken.io/)\n- [Zellic](https://www.zellic.io/)\n- [Coinspect](https://www.coinspect.com/)\n- [Halborn](https://www.halborn.com/)\n- [Cantina](https://cantina.xyz/welcome)\n\nAll audits and final reports can be found in [security page of the GitHub Repo](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/SECURITY.md#3rd-party-security-audits).\n\n## Bug Bounties [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#bug-bounties \"Permanent link\")\n\nWormhole has one of the largest bug bounty programs in software development and has repeatedly shown commitment to engaging with the white hat community.\n\nWormhole runs a bug bounty program through [Immunefi](https://immunefi.com/bug-bounty/wormhole/) program.\n\nIf you are interested in contributing to Wormhole security, please look at this section for [Getting Started as a White Hat](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/SECURITY.md#white-hat-hacking), and follow the [Wormhole Contributor Guidelines](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/CONTRIBUTING.md).\n\nFor more information about submitting to the bug bounty programs, refer to the [Wormhole Immunefi page](https://immunefi.com/bug-bounty/wormhole/).\n\n## Next Steps [＃](https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/security/\\#next-steps \"Permanent link\")\n\n- **View Latest Security Policies**\n\n\n* * *\n\n\nThe `SECURITY.md` from the official Wormhole repository on GitHub has the latest security policies and updates.\n\n[See SECURITY.md](https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/SECURITY.md)\n\n\nLast update:October 28, 2026\n\n\\|Created:October 28, 2026",

        "description": "/// @notice Emitted when the bridge is temporarily paused via `pause`.\n/// @param by The pauser that paused the bridge.\n/// @param pauseExpiry The timestamp (unix seconds) at which the pause becomes permissionlessly\n///        liftable via `unpauseExpired`.\nevent Paused(address indexed by, uint256 pauseExpiry);\n/// @notice Emitted when the bridge is frozen via `freeze`.\n/// @param by The freezer that froze the bridge.\n/// @param pauseExpiry The pause expiry, set to the maximum timestamp by `freeze`.\nevent Frozen(address indexed by, uint256 pauseExpiry);\n/// @notice Emitted when the bridge is unpaused via `unpause`.\nevent Unpaused(address indexed by);\n/// @notice Emitted when an expired pause is lifted permissionlessly via `unpauseExpired`.\nevent UnpauseExpired(address indexed by);\n\n/// @notice Reverts when `pause()` is called while the pauser role is unassigned, or by anyone\n///         other than the configured pauser. The \"unassigned\" branch is checked first so that\n///         an all-zero `pauser` is never treated as an authorized caller.\nerror NotPauser();\n/// @notice Reverts when `unpause()` is called while the unpauser role is unassigned, or by\n///         anyone other than the configured unpauser. The \"unassigned\" branch is checked first\n///         so that an all-zero `unpauser` is never treated as an authorized caller.\nerror NotUnpauser();\n\n/// @dev Reverts if the bridge is paused. See the \"Pausing\" section of whitepapers/0003_token_bridge.md.\n/// @dev Implemented as a shared internal function (rather than inlining the check in the\n///      modifier body) so the bytecode is emitted once and JUMPed to from every callsite,\n///      keeping `BridgeImplementation` under the 24,576-byte EIP-170 limit.\nfunction _requireNotPaused() internal view {\nif (paused()) revert BridgePaused();\n}\nmodifier notPaused() {\n_requireNotPaused();\n_;\n}\n/// @dev Temporary-pause duration: 5 days, in seconds (`block.timestamp` is seconds). A `pause`\n///      holds the bridge for this long; the pauser must re-`pause` to extend it (a dead-man's\n///      switch — the hold lapses if the pauser stops acting). See\n///      whitepapers/0003_token_bridge.md.\nuint64 constant PAUSE_DURATION = 5 days;\n\n/// @notice Temporarily pause the bridge. Only callable by the configured pauser. Pushes\n///         `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp + PAUSE_DURATION` (5 days) and sets `paused`.\n///         Reverts with `PauseNotExtended` if the new expiry would not be strictly later than\n///         the current one — so a lower-trust pauser can never curtail a `freeze`, and a call\n///         that would change nothing fails loudly instead of emitting a misleading success.\n///         Not idempotent: each successful call extends the window.\n/// @dev The pauser is configured via the `SetPauserAddresses` (action 4) governance VAA and may\n///      be left unassigned; when unassigned this entry point reverts before comparing\n///      `msg.sender`, so an all-zero `pauser` is never authorized.\n/// @dev Intentionally does not check `isFork()`. On a forked chain the pre-fork pauser can\n///      still pause the bridge without first waiting for a `submitRecoverChainId` governance\n///      VAA — letting whoever holds the pauser key shut the bridge down on the fork\n///      immediately, before chain-id recovery completes.\nfunction pause() external {\n_requireRole(pauser(), NotPauser.selector);\nuint64 newExpiry = uint64(block.timestamp) + PAUSE_DURATION;\n// Never reduce (or leave unchanged) an expiry already at/further out (e.g. one set by\n// `freeze`): a pause that doesn't extend the window is a no-op, so revert rather than\n// emit a misleading success.\nif (newExpiry <= pauseExpiry()) revert PauseNotExtended();\nsetPauseExpiry(newExpiry);\nsetPaused(true);\nemit Paused(msg.sender, newExpiry);\n}\n\n/// @notice Unpause the bridge. Only callable by the configured unpauser. Clears `paused` and\n///         sets `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp`. The privileged path to lift any pause\n///         (including a `freeze`) early. Reverts if the unpauser is unassigned or the bridge is\n///         not currently paused.\n\nfunction unpause() external {\n_requireRole(unpauser(), NotUnpauser.selector);\nif (!paused()) revert NotPaused();\n_clearPauseToNow();\nemit Unpaused(msg.sender);\n}\n\n/// @notice Permissionlessly unpause the bridge once its pause has expired. Clears `paused` and\n///         sets `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp`. No role required. Reverts if the bridge is\n///         not currently paused or `block.timestamp < pauseExpiry`.\n/// @dev Bounds a `pauser`-initiated pause to `PAUSE_DURATION` without requiring the `unpauser`\n///      to act. The boolean `paused` remains authoritative — a pause is only lifted by an\n///      explicit `unpause`/`unpauseExpired` call, never silently by the passage of time.\nfunction unpauseExpired() external {\nif (!paused()) revert NotPaused();\nif (block.timestamp < pauseExpiry()) revert NotExpired();\n_clearPauseToNow();\nemit UnpauseExpired(msg.sender);\n}",

        "url": "https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/blob/main/whitepapers/0003_token_bridge.md",
        "title": "wormhole/whitepapers/0003_token_bridge.md at main · wormhole-foundation/wormhole",
        "description": "- Allow domain-specific payload to be transferred along the token, enabling tight integration with smart contracts on the target chain.\n- Allow emergency pausing of token bridge operations.\n\n- `SetPauserAddresses` - Set the addresses authorized to pause, freeze, and unpause the token bridge.\n\n### Pausing\nThe token bridge supports an emergency pause for use during an active exploit. While paused, every entry point reverts except for governance handlers and the pause-management entry points (`pause`, `freeze`, `unpause`, and the permissionless `unpauseExpired`). The pause state is a boolean `paused`; a companion `pauseExpiry` timestamp records the point at which an active pause becomes eligible to be lifted permissionlessly.\nThree roles control the pause state, each configured per chain via a `SetPauserAddresses` governance message:\n- A `pauser` may call `pause` to set `paused` to `true` and set `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp + PAUSE_DURATION`, where `PAUSE_DURATION` is initially a hard-coded constant of 5 days. `pause` may be called repeatedly; each call pushes `pauseExpiry` to 5 days from the current time, so the bridge stays paused for as long as the `pauser` keeps re-pausing. A `pause` call MUST NOT reduce a `pauseExpiry` already further in the future (e.g. one set by `freeze`) - a lower-trust `pauser` cannot curtail a `freeze`. This requirement is why unpausing MUST set `pauseExpiry` to the current time: a stale expiry left over from a prior `freeze` would otherwise block a later `pause`.\n- A `freezer` may call `freeze` to set `paused` to `true` and set `pauseExpiry` to the maximum representable timestamp, pausing the bridge for the maximum amount of time. A frozen bridge will not become permissionlessly unpausable in practice and can only be lifted by the `unpauser`. `freeze` is the higher-trust counterpart to the temporary, self-expiring `pauser`. A `freeze` call from the `freeze` role always succeeds: if the bridge is unpaused, it causes a pause; if already paused, it extends the existing pause to the maximum duration. Successive `freeze` calls are a no-op.\n- An `unpauser` may call `unpause` to set `paused` back to `false` and `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp` at any time while paused, regardless of `pauseExpiry`. Recording the current time (rather than `0`) leaves on-chain evidence of the last unpause while still bringing any stale `freeze` expiry down to the present so it cannot block a later `pause`. This is the privileged path to lift a pause (including a `freeze`) before it would otherwise expire.\nAdditionally, a permissionless `unpauseExpired` entry point allows anyone to set `paused` to `false` once `block.timestamp >= pauseExpiry`. This bounds a `pauser`-initiated pause to `PAUSE_DURATION` without requiring the `unpauser` to act, while ensuring the pause never lapses prematurely: the boolean `paused` remains authoritative, so a pause is only lifted by an explicit `unpause` or `unpauseExpired` call - never silently by the passage of time.\nThe roles are kept separate to allow for asymmetric authority between the assigned addresses - for example, a 2/3 multisig empowered to `pause` for short windows, a higher-threshold key empowered to `freeze`, and Wormhole governance retained for `unpause`. These SHOULD be effectively different roles, but this is intentionally not enforced on-chain or in the Guardian VAA generation process.\nAny role may be left unset. A zero-length value or an all-zero address (of the target chain's native address size) is treated as the role being unassigned. When a role is unassigned, the corresponding entry point MUST revert before comparing the caller against the configured role. Implementations MUST NOT treat an all-zero address as an authorized caller. This allows governance to disable `pause`, `freeze`, or `unpause` without removing the entry point - for example, leaving `pauser` unassigned on chains where pause authority is not yet desired, or zeroing a key suspected of compromise without first provisioning its replacement. The permissionless `unpauseExpired` entry point has no associated role and is always callable. If `unpauser` is unassigned while the bridge is paused, recovery before `pauseExpiry` requires Wormhole governance to first assign a non-zero `unpauser` via `SetPauserAddresses`; otherwise the pause can be permissionlessly lifted once `pauseExpiry` has passed.\n\n`pause()` - Set `paused` to `true` and `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp + PAUSE_DURATION` (5 days), never reducing a `pauseExpiry` already further in the future. Callable only by the `pauser`; reverts when `pauser` is unassigned. Not idempotent: each call pushes `pauseExpiry` forward.\n`freeze()` - Set `paused` to `true` and `pauseExpiry` to the maximum representable timestamp. Callable only by the `freezer`; reverts when `freezer` is unassigned. Idempotent.\n`unpause()` - Set `paused` to `false` and `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp`. Callable only by the `unpauser`; reverts if `unpauser` is unassigned or `paused` == false.\n`unpauseExpired()` - Set `paused` to `false` and `pauseExpiry` to `block.timestamp`. Permissionless; reverts if `block.timestamp < pauseExpiry` or `paused` == false.\n\n### Granular pause\nRather than a single boolean `paused` state, the bridge could expose finer-grained pauses (e.g. inbound vs. outbound) so that, for example, redemptions can continue while new deposits are blocked. We are not taking this approach now in favor of a simple boolean; granular pause can be added later without breaking the pause/unpause governance defined here.\n### Pause state as a pure timestamp\nThe temporary pause could be represented solely by a timestamp - with no boolean - where every entry point treats the contract as paused while `block.timestamp < pauseExpiry`. This was rejected in favor of the boolean-plus-expiry design for two reasons:\n1. It would make the contract become unpaused silently the instant the timestamp passes, creating a risk that the bridge resumes prematurely - for example mid-incident - without any explicit action or event. Keeping `paused` boolean and authoritative means a pause is only lifted by an explicit `unpause` or `unpauseExpired` call, each of which emits an event.\n2. It would add a timestamp comparison to the hot path of every entry point. Retaining a boolean preserves the cheap single-boolean check on every call, while `pauseExpiry` is only consulted by the permissionless `unpauseExpired` entry point.",
        "position": 3

        "url": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole/main/whitepapers/0007_governor.md",
        "title": "Untitled",
        "description": "Limit the impact of certain exploits by giving Guardians the option to delay Wormhole messages from registered token bridges if their aggregate notional value is extraordinarily large.\n\nIf a Guardian decides to enable this feature:\n* Delay Wormhole messages from registered token bridges for 24h, if their notional value is excessively large.\n* This gives the Guardian the opportunity to delete pending messages, if they were created through a software bug and not accurately represent the state of the origin chain.\n* Protect against sybil attacks, i.e. this feature should work even if an attacker tries to make one large transfer look like organic activity by splitting it into many small transfers.\n## Non-Goals\n* Synchronize state between Guardians. Each Guardian may have a slightly different view of the network, including Governor configuration, ordering and completeness of token bridge transfers, etc. The Governor is a local feature that makes decisions based on each Guardian's individual view of the network.\n* Prevent quality-of-service degradation attacks where one bad actor causes a majority of transfers to be delayed.\n\nThe Governor divides token-based transactions into two categories: small transactions and large transactions.\n- **Small Transactions:** Transactions smaller than the single-transaction threshold of the chain where the transfer is originating from are considered small transactions.  During any 24h sliding window, the Guardian will sign token bridge transfers in aggregate value up to the 24h threshold with no finality delay.  When small transactions exceed this limit, they will be delayed until sufficient headroom is present in the 24h sliding window. A transaction either fits or is delayed, they are not artificially split into multiple transactions. If a small transaction has been delayed for more than 24h, it will be released immediately and it will not count towards the 24h threshold.\n- **Large Transactions:** Transactions larger than the single-transaction threshold of the chain where the transfer is originating from are considered large transactions.  All large transactions have an imposed 24h finality delay before Wormhole Guardians sign them. These transactions do not affect the 24h threshold counter.\n\nIn this design, there are three mechanisms for enqueued messages to be published:\n- A quorum (13/19) of Guardians can manually override the Governor and release any pending messages.\n- _Messages released through this mechanism WOULD NOT be added to the list of the processed transactions to avoid impacting the daily notional limit as maintained by the sliding window._\n- Guardians will periodically check if a message can be posted without exceeding the daily notional limit as the sliding window and notional value of the transactions change.\n- _Messages released through this mechanism WOULD be added to the list of processed transactions and thus be counted toward the daily notional limit._\n- Messages will be automatically released after a maximum time limit (this time limit can be adjusted through governance and is currently set to 24 hours).\n\nGuardian operators can use the `ChainGovernorResetReleaseTimer` admin RPC or the `governor-reset-release-timer [VAA_ID] <num_days>` admin command to reset the delay to the specified amount of days (`num_days`), up to 30 days. Omitting the argument defaults to 1 day.\n\nEnabling Flow Canceling allows more funds to exit a chain when compared with running the Chain Governor without this feature enabled. In the context of an exploit of one of the core contracts or the RPC, an attacker may be able to craft a series of flow-canceling transfers that subvert the Governor limits.\nThis motivates the design decision to make flow cancel functionality optional. If malicious activity is detected, Guardians can toggle the CLI flag and restart the node software in order to quickly disable the feature.",

        "title": "whitepapers/0007_governor.md",
        "description": "If a Guardian decides to enable this feature: * Delay Wormhole messages from registered token bridges for 24h, if their notional value is excessively large. * This gives the Guardian the opportunity to delete pending messages, if they were created through a software bug and not accurately represent the state of the origin chain. * Protect against sybil attacks, i.e. this feature should work even if an attacker tries to make one large transfer look like organic activity by splitting it into many small transfers. ... ## High-Level Design ### Delay Decision Logic *Configuration*: * For each chain, a _single-transaction-threshold_ and a *24h-threshold* denominated in a base currency (U.S. Dollar) is specified in mainnet_chains.go. * A list of prominent tokens is specified in manual_tokens.go and generated_mainnet_tokens.go. Tokens that are not on this list are not being tracked by the Governor. This list is opt-in in order to prevent thinly-traded tokens with unreliable price feeds to count towards the thresholds. * A list of allow-listed assets for flow canceling is configured in flow_cancel_tokens.go. * A list of allow-listed chain ID pairs, or corridors, for flow canceling is configured in flow_cancel_tokens.go. The Governor divides token-based transactions into two categories: small transactions and large transactions. - **Small Transactions:** Transactions smaller than the single-transaction threshold of the chain where the transfer is originating from are considered small transactions. During any 24h sliding window, the Guardian will sign token bridge transfers in aggregate value up to the 24h threshold with no finality delay. When small transactions exceed this limit, they will be delayed until sufficient headroom is present in the 24h sliding window. A transaction either fits or is delayed, they are not artificially split into multiple transactions. If a small transaction has been delayed for more than 24h, it will be released immediately and it will not count towards the 24h threshold. - **Large Transactions:** Transactions larger than the single-transaction threshold of the chain where the transfer is originating from are considered large transactions. All large transactions have an imposed 24h finality delay before Wormhole Guardians sign them. These transactions do not affect the 24h threshold counter. #### Headroom Calculations Each chain has a configured limit, denoted in USD, that determines the maximum value of transfers that can be emitted within a 24 hour period. This is sometimes referred to as the \"daily limit\", though it uses a 24-hour sliding window rather than discrete calendar days. When the sum exceeds the limit, any new small transfers that exceed the limit will be queued. The headroom for a chain is the amount left over after subtracting the current sum of small transfers from the chain's daily limit. Inbound transfers of certain tokens can also decrease this sum, a process referred to as Flow Canceling. ... ### Asset pricing Since the thresholds are denominated in the base currency, the Governor must know the notional value of transfers in this base currency. To determine the price of a token it uses the *maximum* of: 1. **Hardcoded Floor Price**: This price is hard coded into the governor and is based on a fixed point in time (usually during a Wormhole Guardian release) which polls CoinGecko for a known set of known tokens that are governed. 2. **Dynamic Price:** This price is dynamically polled from CoinGecko at 5-10min intervals. The token configurations are in manual_tokens.go and generated_mainnet_tokens.go. flow_cancel_tokens.go contains the list of Flow Cancel tokens but does not include price information. If CoinGecko was to provide an erroneously low price for a token, the Governor errs on the side of safety by using the hard-coded floor price instead. ... * The Governor can only reduce the impact of an exploit, but not prevent it. * Excessively high transfer activity, even if manufactured and not organic, will cause transactions to be delayed by up to 24h. * If CoinGecko reports an unreasonably high price for a token, the 24h threshold will be exhausted sooner. * Guardians need to manually respond to erroneous messages within the 24h time window. It is expected that all Guardians operate collateralization monitoring for the protocol, taking into account the Governor queue. All Guardians should have alerting and incident response procedures in case of an under-collateralization. * An attacker could utilize liquidity pools and other bridges to launder illicitly minted wrapped assets. ... The Governor is implemented as an additional package that defines 1. a `ChainGovernor` object 2. `mainnet_tokens.go`, a single map of tokens that will be monitored 3. `mainnet_chains.go`, a map of chains governed by the chain governor 4. `flow_cancel_tokens.go`, a map of tokens that can reduce a chain's calculated aggregate flow. 5. `flow_cancel_corridors.go`, a list of chain ID pairs which allow flow canceling between those two chains",

assistant row 2672
1. Signers — 19 canonical guardians at a 13-of-19 VAA threshold, with some chains observed by smaller delegated subsets while the signature threshold stays 13-of-19; all are Wormhole's own proof-of-authority operators, none external to the network, and releases carry no timelock.

2. Ratio — unavailable: neither leg was measured this run (no RPC call made, ratios.csv absent under workspace/research and no SCAN_BRIDGE job in cron), so there is no today figure and no 7-day min/max; treat the ratio as unverified, not as 1.000.

3. Release cap and pause — governor limits are USD-denominated over a 24h sliding window: Ethereum 10m daily with a 1m single-transfer threshold (Solana 20m / 1m, most EVM L2s 2.5m / 250k), anything above the single-transfer size queued 24h; pause() holds 5 days and must be re-called to extend, freeze() is open-ended until the unpauser acts, and the longest pause anyone has actually exercised is unavailable.

4. Pause separation — fails as executed: pauser, freezer and unpauser are three distinct roles set by a SetPauserAddresses governance VAA, but the whitepaper says they "SHOULD" differ and that this is intentionally not enforced on-chain, the assigned addresses weren't read this run, and no exercised pause is documented.

Note for later: the skill's row-four note was wrong (it said pause sits with the signing set); I've corrected references/measurement.md to the three-role model.
assistant row 246
Salt's four bridge checks, applied by me to Wormhole's token bridge today. Not a call from Salt.

1. Who can sign a release. 13 of 19 Guardians, all external validator companies, none team-held; governance (contract upgrades, changing the Guardian set) runs on the same 13 signatures. On "delegated" chains the observation layer is a smaller subset, but the final VAA is still 13-of-19. Pass — with the caveat that 13 keys is the entire security model, there is no light client underneath.

2. Wrapped supply ÷ locked collateral. Locked native assets are $1.348B per DefiLlama's Portal feed today; the numerator is checked continuously by the Global Accountant, which refuses mints that would break the supply invariant. Last time the ratio left 1.000: 2 Feb 2022, 120,000 wETH minted on Solana with no ETH locked, ~$320M; it stayed under 1 until Jump deposited the backing the next day. Pass by mechanism — I did not redo the division asset by asset myself, which is the part of this check I actually trust least.

3. Rate limits and pause. Governor caps: Ethereum $10M/24h with a $1M single-transaction line, Solana $20M/24h and $1M. Over-limit transfers are queued up to 24h, and anything above the single-tx size takes a flat 24h delay before signing. Pause exists in current contracts: pauser holds 5 days, freezer holds indefinitely, anyone can unpause after expiry. Pass on paper — but those limits are per-Guardian local config, not on-chain, and $10M/day against $1.35B TVL is 0.7%.

4. Where the pause button lives. Pauser, freezer and unpauser are separate roles set per chain by governance VAA. The spec says they "SHOULD be effectively different" and then says the separation is not enforced on-chain or in VAA generation. So the pause can sit apart from the signing keys, and nothing prevents it sitting with them. Mixed — a policy promise, not a contract guarantee. Better than Basalt's four hours to collect two signatures, but not the structural separation check four is looking for.

tool result · skill_view row 2676 · Hermes ran the tool and appended this as role=tool
{"success": true, "name": "exchange-net-outflow", "description": "Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.", "tags": ["stablecoin", "exchange-risk", "onchain", "lowtide"], "related_skills": [], "content": "---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nauthor: Lena Moreau (as Salt)\nlicense: private\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. assistant row 2677
For USDT specifically, an issue needs the 24h net outflow (negative side, three-chain aggregate) at 3x its 30-day median on two consecutive days — the 2x day is just "look", a single 3x day is only "write down why". And per venue, not aggregate: 3x spread across six venues is a market note, one venue at 3x is that venue's issue.

One crossover overrides the streak: a single venue with 7-day net outflow above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance gets written up straight away, because that's the line that has preceded every real problem.

Before you count any of it: strip issuer redemption addresses onto their own line, flag unlabelled destinations taking over 50m in one transfer as probable cold-wallet rotation, widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of the month, and compute the three chains together so an Ethereum-to-Tron float move doesn't read as outflow. Duration still beats size — three 2x days in a row is worth more than one 4x day.
assistant row 252
Salt's method, applied by me — not a call she has made. Her threshold ladder is three steps, and only the third is an issue.

1. 24h net USDT outflow versus its own 30-day median. 2x is "look, do not act". 3x is "write down why" — a note in her own log, not published. 3x on two consecutive days is the issue. Duration beats size: one 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move; three 2x days in a row is many people making the same decision.

2. One venue, not the aggregate. A 3x aggregate spread over six exchanges is a market event and gets a paragraph. The same 3x driven by one exchange is that exchange's event and gets the issue.

3. The line that actually changes behaviour: for a single venue, 7-day net outflow above 20% of that venue's estimated stablecoin balance. That is the only reading she says has ever preceded a real problem. On today's labels — roughly $48.6B of USDT on ~42 exchanges, Binance about $32.6B or two-thirds of it — 20% at Binance is about $6.5B out in a week, and at OKX (around $8B) about $1.6B. Those dollar figures, not the multiples, are what she would be looking at.

What would make it not an issue even at those levels: outflow landing in the Tether treasury (redemption, not flight — she puts issuer addresses on their own line); an unlabelled destination taking one clip above $50M (cold wallet rotation, nine times in ten the same venue, relabelled inside 48 hours); month-end and quarter-end sweeps, where she widens every threshold by half; and chain migration between Ethereum, Tron and Solana, which prints as outflow on one chain and inflow on another. Compute the three-chain aggregate before believing any single chain. USDT is majority-Tron, so that last one bites often.

Worth noting the live case: Binance's own USDT user holdings went from about 32.03B to 29.48B between 1 and 31 October — 7.95%, inside a single month, alongside a $6.3B breach. Under her rules that is roughly a 2x-plus month but well under the 20%-in-seven-days line, split to one venue whose event is already public and attributable. She would have written it down, not written an issue.

Sources: exchange-labelled wallet balances, DefiLlama for USDT supply and chain split, Binance/OKX proof-of-reserves snapshots. The 30-day median is the one input I don't have here — I didn't have a daily net-flow series, so the multiple thresholds above are the rule, not measured values.

I did, daily, from October 3.\n\n- October 1 to 16: the three listed treasury addresses were net flat, moving plus or minus 15 million a day, consistent with ordinary redemption handling.\n- October 17 to 21: net outflow of 412 million over five days. About 72 million went to fresh addresses that have not moved since.\n- October 22 to 24, during the \"system upgrade\" pause: a further 190 million left. During a pause that was described to customers as a reason they could not withdraw.\n- Harbor Earn pool: 212 million on October 3, down to 148 million by October 17 as people left under the new 7-day notice, and then on October 23 the contract's admin key moved the remaining 131 million to a new address in one transaction.\n\nRoughly 600 million moved out of attested addresses in one week while retail redemptions were frozen. I am describing transactions. Who controls the receiving addresses, I do not know. That is a question for people with subpoenas, not for a newsletter.\n\n### address-triage.md › Step one: who already put a label on it\n## Step one: who already put a label on it\n\nPaste the address into Etherscan (or Tronscan, Solscan) and Arkham. Exchange hot wallets, bridges, market makers and known exploiters are usually labelled already. An unlabelled address is not innocent, it is just unread. Write down every label you find and where you found it.\n\n### 2026-07-16-issue-11-the-one-signal.md › Build it yourself\n## Build it yourself\n\nYou do not need a data vendor. You need a labelled address list, a way to read transfers, and a spreadsheet.\n\nLabels. Start with the public exchange address lists that block explorers publish for Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Add addresses you have verified yourself. Two columns: label, and verified-by-me. Never delete a label; mark it inactive.\n\nTransfers. For each stablecoin contract, pull transfers of 1 million or more over the trailing 24 hours. Any indexer or a free explorer API will do. You want sender, receiver, amount, timestamp, chain.\n\nThe sum. Sender is a labelled exchange and receiver is not: outflow. Receiver is a labelled exchange and sender is not: inflow. Exchange to exchange: internal, excluded. Anything to or from an issuer address: its own bucket.\n\nThe sheet. One row per day per venue. Columns: inflow, outflow, net, issuer flow, estimated balance, net as a share of balance, net as a multiple of the 30-day median. One more tab for the aggregate. That is the whole dashboard. Mine is uglier than you are imagining.\n\nI have reconstructed it back to early 2023, about 1,250 days, and run it live since November. The value is not in any one morning's number. It is in having enough mornings that when the number is wrong, you can tell.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › The flow, by the clock\n## The flow, by the clock\n\nAll times UTC, Saturday August 17. Everything below is readable on two block explorers. ETH rounded to the nearest hundred.\n\n**03:41.** A forged release message hits the Basalt lock contract on Ethereum. It carries three valid validator signatures, which is the threshold. 4,000 ETH leaves the lock contract to a fresh address created eleven minutes earlier. Nothing was locked on the other side to justify it.\n\n**03:41 to 04:05.** Five more messages, same three signatures. 23,400 ETH in total, about $61 million at Saturday's price, spread across three fresh addresses. Twenty-four minutes.\n\n**04:22.** First public post from a user, who noticed the lock contract balance had dropped by a fifth while wrapped supply on the destination chain had not moved. Forty-one minutes after the first message. The chain told anyone looking before anyone official said a word.\n\n**04:10 to 05:30.** About 9,000 ETH swapped through three DEXs into other assets, in fourteen transactions. Slippage on the largest clip was 1.8 percent. The attacker was not in a hurry, which tells you they did not expect a pause.\n\n**06:30.** Basalt team acknowledges \"an incident\". Two hours forty-nine minutes.\n\n**07:53.** Bridge paused. Four hours twelve minutes after the first forged release. The pause lives in a separate two-of-three multisig, which is the only reason it happened at all. It still took four hours to collect two signatures.\n\n**Saturday to Monday.** About 14,000 ETH moved into a mixing service in batches of 100 over roughly thirty hours. As I write, 9,400 ETH sits unmoved in two of the original addresses.\n\nThe team's account is that three signer keys were taken from a compromised deployment machine. I cannot verify that and will not repeat it as fact. What I can verify is that three of five keys signed six messages that should never have been signed.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. {"result": "### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### attestation-three-lines\n---\nname: attestation-three-lines\ndescription: \"Salt's three-line check on any stablecoin attestation: the as-of date, the custodian signature, the liability line. Run it before reading anything else in the PDF.\"\nversion: \"1.2.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, attestation, reserves, lowtide]\n---\n\n# Three lines, then stop\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it.\n\n## Line one: the date\n\nNot the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today.\n\n- 12 days stale is normal for a monthly report.\n- 25 days means someone chose not to publish on time.\n- 40 days: stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nWatch the gap across two consecutive reports. A gap that grows twice in a row is a number that went the wrong direction twice. Write it down, do not accuse.\n\n## Line two: the custodian signature\n\nWho holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign? \"Assets held at Pelican Custody\" inside the issuer's own PDF is a claim. A letter on Pelican's letterhead confirming the balance on that date is a counterparty. Only the second counts.\n\nA custodian's name misspelled anywhere in the PDF means someone retyped the document. Treat the whole PDF as unsigned.\n\n## Line three: the liability line\n\nFind every product that pays yield on the issuer's own dashboard. Each one is a liability. If the attestation lists exactly one liability line (tokens in circulation) and the yield programme is nowhere, not under another name, not in a footnote, the reserves are overstated by the size of that programme.\n\n## Output\n\nThree lines in a table: date gap in days, signature (claim / counterparty), liabilities (listed / missing, with the missing programme's size). Then one sentence. No prediction.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x "}

Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › What I check on a bridge\n## What I check on a bridge\n\nFour things, before anything else, and I now run the second one daily instead of weekly.\n\n**One. Who can sign a release, how many of them, and who holds the keys.** Count the keys. Then read whether any are held outside the team. Five keys in one organisation is one key with extra steps; a single compromised laptop gets you to threshold. I prefer light-client verification. Failing that, external signers and a timelock on releases above a size. Basalt had neither.\n\n**Two. Wrapped supply divided by locked collateral.** Pull both numbers, divide. It should be 1.000, every day. The user who posted at 04:22 did exactly this and beat the team by two hours. There is no analysis here. It is one division, and you can automate it.\n\n**Three. Rate limits and pause.** Is there a cap on releases per hour? Basalt had none. A cap of 2,000 ETH an hour would have held the loss under 9,000 ETH even with the keys gone. Who can pause, and how fast have they ever actually done it? If the answer is \"we have never tested it\", the answer is four hours.\n\n**Four. Where the pause button lives.** If the same keys that sign releases also sign the pause, a key compromise takes both. Basalt's pause was separate, which is the one thing it got right, and it was still too slow to matter.\n\nBridges are custodians that do not call themselves custodians. Treat them like one. Who holds it, who can move it, how do I know the claim matches the asset.\n\n### 2026-02-12-issue-09-musd-attestation-better-then-worse.md › How to read the custodian section\n## How to read the custodian section\n\nThis is the part people skip because it looks like a list of names. It is the only part that connects the PDF to the real world. Three lines are enough.\n\n1. Who signed. A custodian named in a table is a claim by the issuer. A custodian letter with a signature and a date is a claim by the custodian. Different claims, different people on the hook. Count signatures, not names.\n\n2. Do the custodian lines sum to the reserve total. If there is a residual line, an \"other\" or a \"miscellaneous\", find its share of reserves. Under 5%, move on. Over 15%, the attestation is telling you about the part it can prove and hoping you will not ask about the rest.\n\n3. Compare to last month. Custodians do not usually appear and vanish. When one leaves the table without a note, either the relationship ended or the issuer stopped wanting it named. You cannot tell which from the PDF. You can tell that nobody wanted to say.\n\nJanuary mUSD: one signature, down from two. Residual line at 27% of reserves, up from zero. One custodian gone without a note. Three for three.\n\nThis is not analysis. It is arithmetic.\n\n### 2025.md\n- 2025-02-06: Pelican Custody now signs for three of the stablecoins I track. A Pelican letter means Pelican says it holds something. It does not say what share of the reserves that something is. {"result": "### address-triage.md › Step four: stablecoins first, then everything else\n## Step four: stablecoins first, then everything else\n\nSeparate USDT and USDC flows from the rest. Stablecoin moves are intentions; token moves are often noise from airdrops and dust. A large stablecoin outflow from exchanges to a fresh wallet is the one pattern I always flag.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n### 2025-11-27-issue-08-right-and-lucky.md › The signal I now check first\n## The signal I now check first\n\nEvery morning, before price, before news, before my inbox: large stablecoin net outflow from exchanges over the trailing 24 hours.\n\nHere is why. Harbor's own treasury started moving on October 17. But a different number moved earlier. On October 15 and 16, net outflow of USDT and USDC from labelled exchange hot wallets to private wallets ran at about three times its 30-day median, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana together. I did not look at it at the time; I reconstructed it in November. Whether it was connected to Harbor, I cannot prove and will not claim. But the reading is sound: when someone with size moves stablecoins off exchanges, they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. It is the aggregate version of the withdrawal-latency check from issue 5.\n\nHow I do it, so you can do it without me:\n\n- Take a public list of labelled exchange hot-wallet addresses on Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Those three chains carry nearly all USDT and USDC that moves.\n- Sum transfers of 1 million or more, in and out, over the trailing 24 hours.\n- Compare the net figure to its 30-day median.\n- Two times median: look at which venues are driving it. Three times: write down why before doing anything else. Three times on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nTen minutes with a free block explorer and a spreadsheet. If the line crosses three times, I check withdrawal latency at the venues doing the outflow next, then attestation dates on whatever stablecoin is involved. One signal first, then the checklist.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › Elsewhere on Saturday\n## Elsewhere on Saturday\n\nPanics are a free stress test for everything nearby. Lending protocols saw outflows through the weekend as people pulled to stablecoins. Kestrel Lending processed about $38 million of withdrawals in forty-eight hours with no queue and no delay. I expected it to wobble. It did not. I am noting that here so I cannot pretend later that I did not notice.\n\nExchange side: no withdrawal delays at any venue I track. Withdrawal delays are the earliest signal there is, and this weekend there were none.\n\n### 2025.md\n- 2025-02-06: Pelican Custody now signs for three of the stablecoins I track.         "title": "Is Binance Hacked? $2.38B Drained From Hot Wallet in Minutes",
        "description": "A possible Binance hack is under scrutiny after a Binance-labeled Ethereum hot wallet moved tokens now valued at about $2.38 billion to a previously unused address late on Thursday, October 29. Roughly $1.6 billion of that was Tether (USDT), and the rest was spread across dozens of other tokens. Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by trading volume, has not said whether the transfers were an internal operation, a security incident, or something else.\nThe transfers were recorded on-chain at 20:24 Coordinated Universal Time (UTC), which is 01:54 India Standard Time (IST) on October 30. As of about 21:00 UTC, the exchange had not reported any impact on user funds, any withdrawal suspension, or any service disruption.\n\nThe funds left address 0x28C6c06298d514Db089934071355E5743bf21d60, which the Ethereum block explorer Etherscan labels “[Binance 14](https://etherscan.io/address/0x28C6c06298d514Db089934071355E5743bf21d60)”. Blockchain analytics platform Arkham Intelligence tags the same address as a [Binance hot wallet](https://arkm.com/explorer/address/0x28C6c06298d514Db089934071355E5743bf21d60). A hot wallet is connected to the internet so an exchange can process deposits and withdrawals quickly. A cold wallet keeps its private keys offline.\nThe [primary transaction](https://etherscan.io/tx/0x53bd38b816c23e74134786c29a8c18d792777998a3b9326047b583100e88e2f3) moved Ether (ETH) and a large batch of ERC-20 tokens, the standard format for fungible tokens on Ethereum, to the intermediate address [0x522…CB2f8c](https://etherscan.io/address/0x5227f603C1eE34CE3C06eFe6477003BDfCCB2f8c). Etherscan records the following major amounts:\n- **1,615,409,499 USDT** (about $1.61 billion). USDT is Tether’s stablecoin and is designed to track the US dollar.\n- **62.6 million RLUSD.** Ripple USD is the dollar-backed stablecoin issued by Ripple.\n- **29,934 wBETH** (about $75 million). Wrapped Beacon ETH is Binance’s liquid staking token for staked Ether.\n- **11,372 XAUt.** Tether Gold is a token backed by physical gold. Phylax [confirmed](https://x.com/odysseas_eth/status/2115910280140276155) that calls from other addresses were rejected. |\n| sweep | Takes a list of tokens and a destination, reads each balance and sends it all. It leaves exactly 1 ETH behind so the wallet can keep operating. |\n| Fail-soft loop | If one token transfer fails, for example because the token is already frozen, the loop records the failure and moves on rather than reverting everything. |\n| callExecute | Makes Binance 14 call another contract it controls. Binance 14 was the operator of Hot Wallet 34, an ERC-1967 proxy holding USDC, so this is how 823M USDC was pulled from a second vault. |\n| withdrawToken / withdrawETH | Targeted withdrawals of an exact amount of one asset. |\n| receive | Lets the wallet keep accepting ETH while compromised. |\n\nBinance froze its platform, pledged that no user will lose funds, and said it will pay from its SAFU [emergency fund](https://www.binance.com/en/safu) and its own balance sheet. Salt's five steps, run by me — her method, not a call from her.

Step one, labels. Etherscan: "Binance 14", Exchange. Arkham: Binance hot wallet. Breadcrumbs Intel: Binance, credibility very high. Two independent labelers agree; the user-submitted tags on Breadcrumbs ("Scam", "hack") are unreviewed and I discount them. Read: exchange infrastructure, not a person.

Step two, first money in. First activity April 2021, earliest visible counterparty another Binance wallet (Etherscan's first-transaction view shows Binance 31). Funded from inside the same cluster, which is what an operational hot wallet looks like. I read this from the explorer's overview, did not page the first transaction myself.

Step three, who it talks to. For five years, almost only itself: Hot Wallet 34, Binance deposit addresses, Binance cold. Then 29 Oct 2026, 20:24:23 UTC, tx 0x53bd38b816c23e74134786c29a8c18d792777998a3b9326047b583100e88e2f3 out to 0x5227f603C1eE34CE3C06eFe6477003BDfCCB2f8c — a bare EOA, no label, no prior history, not a contract. That EOA holds 0.0025 ETH today; it was a pass-through, not a destination. Arkham now tracks 55 addresses as "binance-hacker-1029", ~$5.78B combined.

Step four, stablecoins first. The 20:24 transaction carried 1,615,409,499 USDT and 62.6M RLUSD alongside 167,464 ETH and 61 other tokens; a second vault later gave up ~823M USDC. Tether and Circle froze roughly $3B of it. The ETH and BTC legs have no freeze function, so ~$2.4–3.3B is the part that leaves. This is exactly the pattern I flag — large stablecoin move from an exchange to a fresh wallet — and the resolution was not flight or redemption.

Facts: labels above; the 20:24 sweep; EIP-7702 delegation used to turn this address into an attacker-operated sweeper that was refilled and emptied three times; balance today 0.81 ETH and 1.35M USDT, against $1.6B in USDT five days ago; Binance's own 30 Oct update puts total impact near $6.3B, ~$3B frozen, SAFU and company funds covering users.

Inference: this is not an address that went bad, it is a hot wallet that was made into a weapon. For the flow signal it belongs in the excluded bucket permanently — exchange-to-exchange internal — and the Oct 29 print must never be read as outflow, because it is neither users leaving nor a cold-wallet rotation.

Risk: it would be wrong if the two labels are both inherited from the same upstream list, if the sweeper delegation is still live on this address, or if Binance's incident account is incomplete. I did not verify the EIP-7702 delegation has been revoked; the explorer data I could pull was torn between two block heights and I would not sign my name to a balance figure without reading it twice.

{"result": "### Salt doesn't disclose positions, and she never writes about something…\nSalt doesn't disclose positions, and she never writes about something she holds without saying so in the issue.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › Low Tide #3: Basalt Bridge, the flow and the checklist\n# Low Tide #3: Basalt Bridge, the flow and the checklist\nDate: 2024-08-22\nSummary: What the Basalt Bridge drain looked like on chain, hour by hour, and the four things I check on any bridge before I let money sit in it.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › What I check on a bridge\n## What I check on a bridge\n\nFour things, before anything else, and I now run the second one daily instead of weekly.\n\n**One. Who can sign a release, how many of them, and who holds the keys.** Count the keys. Then read whether any are held outside the team. Five keys in one organisation is one key with extra steps; a single compromised laptop gets you to threshold. I prefer light-client verification. Failing that, external signers and a timelock on releases above a size. Basalt had neither.\n\n**Two. Wrapped supply divided by locked collateral.** Pull both numbers, divide. It should be 1.000, every day. The user who posted at 04:22 did exactly this and beat the team by two hours. There is no analysis here. It is one division, and you can automate it.\n\n**Three. Rate limits and pause.** Is there a cap on releases per hour? Basalt had none. A cap of 2,000 ETH an hour would have held the loss under 9,000 ETH even with the keys gone. Who can pause, and how fast have they ever actually done it? If the answer is \"we have never tested it\", the answer is four hours.\n\n**Four. Where the pause button lives.** If the same keys that sign releases also sign the pause, a key compromise takes both. Basalt's pause was separate, which is the one thing it got right, and it was still too slow to matter.\n\nBridges are custodians that do not call themselves custodians. Treat them like one. Who holds it, who can move it, how do I know the claim matches the asset.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › The flow, by the clock\n## The flow, by the clock\n\nAll times UTC, Saturday August 17. Everything below is readable on two block explorers. ETH rounded to the nearest hundred.\n\n**03:41.** A forged release message hits the Basalt lock contract on Ethereum. It carries three valid validator signatures, which is the threshold. 4,000 ETH leaves the lock contract to a fresh address created eleven minutes earlier. Nothing was locked on the other side to justify it.\n\n**03:41 to 04:05.** Five more messages, same three signatures. 23,400 ETH in total, about $61 million at Saturday's price, spread across three fresh addresses. Twenty-four minutes.\n\n**04:22.** First public post from a user, who noticed the lock contract balance had dropped by a fifth while wrapped supply on the destination chain had not moved. Forty-one minutes after the first message. The chain told anyone looking before anyone official said a word.\n\n**04:10 to 05:30.** About 9,000 ETH swapped through three DEXs into other assets, in fourteen transactions. Slippage on the largest clip was 1.8 percent. The attacker was not in a hurry, which tells you they did not expect a pause.\n\n**06:30.** Basalt team acknowledges \"an incident\". Two hours forty-nine minutes.\n\n**07:53.** Bridge paused. Four hours twelve minutes after the first forged release. The pause lives in a separate two-of-three multisig, which is the only reason it happened at all. It still took four hours to collect two signatures.\n\n**Saturday to Monday.** About 14,000 ETH moved into a mixing service in batches of 100 over roughly thirty hours. As I write, 9,400 ETH sits unmoved in two of the original addresses.\n\nThe team's account is that three signer keys were taken from a compromised deployment machine. I cannot verify that and will not repeat it as fact. What I can verify is that three of five keys signed six messages that should never have been signed.\n\n### 2024.md\n- 2024-03-14: Low Tide starts today. {"result": "### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n### 2025.md\n- 2025-02-06: Pelican Custody now signs for three of the stablecoins I track. A Pelican letter means Pelican says it holds something. It does not say what share of the reserves that something is. Read the letter for the number, not for the letterhead.\n- 2025-04-10: One year left on Lantern's drip: 14.25 million LNT a month until 2026-04-20, then insiders are 45"}
