{"result": "### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### 2026-04-23-issue-10-lantern-last-row.md › Low Tide #10: Lantern Labs reaches the last row of its table\n# Low Tide #10: Lantern Labs reaches the last row of its table\nDate: 2026-04-23\nSummary: On 2026-04-20 the final tranche of Lantern's team and investor vesting unlocked, the date issue #2 circled two years ago and I re-posted one year ago; here is what price, depth and the borrow market did, what the table predicted, and what it could not.\n\n### 2024-05-09-issue-02-unlock-schedules.md › Lantern Labs, the worked example\n## Lantern Labs, the worked example\n\nLantern Labs launched its L2 token, LNT, on February 20 this year. I counted, because the number matters later.\n\nHere is the schedule, pulled from the vesting contracts and cross-checked against the tokenomics page. Where the two disagreed I used the contract. Everything you need is in them.\n\n### attestation-three-lines\n---\nname: attestation-three-lines\ndescription: \"Salt's three-line check on any stablecoin attestation: the as-of date, the custodian signature, the liability line. Everything you need is in them.\n\n### 2024-05-09-issue-02-unlock-schedules.md › Read the table, skip the prose\n## Read the table, skip the prose\n\nWhitepapers are written to be read. Unlock schedules are written to be filed. Guess which one tells the truth.\n\nA whitepaper tells you what the team wants you to think the token is for. The unlock schedule tells you who gets how many tokens, on what date, and therefore who is able to sell into you and when. One of those documents can be rewritten after launch. The other is in a vesting contract.\n\nIf I can only read one document about a token, I read the unlock table. Usually I only read the unlock table. A project's token unlock schedule matters more than its whitepaper, and I will keep saying that until it stops being true.\n\n### 2024-05-09-issue-02-unlock-schedules.md › Low Tide #2: The unlock table is the whitepaper\n# Low Tide #2: The unlock table is the whitepaper\nDate: 2024-05-09\nSummary: Why a token's unlock schedule tells you more than anything the project wrote about itself, worked through on Lantern Labs with the table and the arithmetic.\n\n### 2024-05-09-issue-02-unlock-schedules.md › How I read any unlock table\n## How I read any unlock table\n\nFour steps, in order, every time.\n\nOne. Find the date when unlocked insider tokens exceed tokens in public hands. For Lantern that is August 20: 16.5 percent insider versus 15 percent public. That is the date the token stops belonging to its holders.\n\nTwo. Divide each monthly unlock by thirty-day volume. Under one day of volume is noise. One to three days is a headwind. Over five and the market is being asked to absorb something it has not shown it can.\n\nThree. Check the docs against the contract, not the other way round. If they disagree, the contract is right and someone wrote the docs carelessly, or not carelessly. Either way you learned something.\n\nFour. For anything labelled community, ecosystem or growth, find the address. Who holds the keys, how many signers, is there a timelock. A 40 percent allocation to a two-of-three multisig is an insider allocation with better branding.\n\nThis is not analysis. It is division.\n\n### 2026-04-23-issue-10-lantern-last-row.md › Low Tide #10: Lantern Labs reaches the last row of its table\n# Low Tide #10: Lantern Labs reaches the last row of its table\nDate: 2026-04-23\nSummary: On 2026-04-20 the final tranche of Lantern's team and investor vesting unlocked, the date issue #2 circled two years ago and I re-posted one year ago; here is what price, depth and the borrow market did, what the table predicted, and what it could not.\n\n### 2026-04-23-issue-10-lantern-last-row.md › The table, again\n## The table, again\n\nIssue #2, May 2024, printed Lantern's unlock table. Five rows. The one that ran longest was the linear vest: 14.25 million LNT a month to team and investors, from September 2024 to April 20, 2026. Last Monday was the last payment. Insiders, 45% of supply, are now fully liquid. Two months earlier, on February 20, the second ecosystem tranche of 100 million landed in the foundation multisig, on schedule.\n\nIn April 2025 I posted the same table with one year left on it and a note: twelve drips remaining, then the only scheduled supply left belongs to the foundation. I said set a reminder. I set one.\n\n### 2024-05-09-issue-02-unlock-schedules.md › The arithmetic\n## The arithmetic\n\nLNT trades at $0.84 today, with ETH around $3,000 and BTC a little above $61,000 for context. Circulating market cap $126 million, fully diluted $840 million. Thirty-day average spot volume across every venue I can find: $7.1 million a day, almost all of it against USDT.\n\nAugust 20, a Tuesday. Team cliff and investor cliff land on the same day. 165 million tokens become transferable. At today's price that is $138.6 million, which is more than the entire current float, which is nineteen and a half days of volume, in one day.\n\nI do not know whether they will sell. I know that they can, and I know what the order book looks like. Both of those are facts. \"They will sell\" is a prediction and I do not make those. But if you are holding LNT on August 19 you should be able to say out loud why a 110 percent increase in sellable supply overnight is fine, and \"the team believes in the vision\" is a sentence from page four, not an answer.\n\nThe cliff is the event. The drip is the regime. From September, 14.25 million tokens a month, about $12 million at today's price, about 1.7 days of volume every month for twenty months. Survivable if usage grows. A slow leak if it does not. Most people price the cliff and forget the drip.\n\nNow the thirty-eight mentions of \"community\". Forty percent of supply is labelled ecosystem. It unlocks in four yearly tranches to a foundation multisig. Not to users. Not to a DAO. To a wallet the foundation controls. Meanwhile the insider 45 percent, team plus investors, is fully liquid by April 2026. I"}

The full policy is in issue 12. Readers can pay for the newsletter; projects cannot pay for coverage.\n\n### 2026-07-16-issue-11-the-one-signal.md › Build it yourself\n## Build it yourself\n\nYou do not need a data vendor. You need a labelled address list, a way to read transfers, and a spreadsheet.\n\nLabels. Start with the public exchange address lists that block explorers publish for Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Add addresses you have verified yourself. Two columns: label, and verified-by-me. Never delete a label; mark it inactive.\n\nTransfers. For each stablecoin contract, pull transfers of 1 million or more over the trailing 24 hours. Any indexer or a free explorer API will do. You want sender, receiver, amount, timestamp, chain.\n\nThe sum. Sender is a labelled exchange and receiver is not: outflow. Receiver is a labelled exchange and sender is not: inflow. Exchange to exchange: internal, excluded. Anything to or from an issuer address: its own bucket.\n\nThe sheet. One row per day per venue. Columns: inflow, outflow, net, issuer flow, estimated balance, net as a share of balance, net as a multiple of the 30-day median. One more tab for the aggregate. That is the whole dashboard. Mine is uglier than you are imagining.\n\nI have reconstructed it back to early 2023, about 1,250 days, and run it live since November. The value is not in any one morning's number. It is in having enough mornings that when the number is wrong, you can tell.\n\n### 2025-05-15-issue-05-tidewater-withdrawal-delay.md › What I told readers to do\n## What I told readers to do\n\nThree lines.\n\n1. Withdraw what you want out now, in small pieces. The first piece tells you the current latency; you learn it without putting everything in one queue.\n2. Do not add funds until the median is back under one hour for two consecutive weeks. Not one good day. Two weeks.\n3. Keep your timestamps and keep sending them. The sheet stays open and I will publish the next window either way, including if it gets better.\n\nWhat I did not tell readers: that Tidewater is going to zero, that they should short anything, or that they should panic. This is not analysis. It is arithmetic, and the arithmetic says the door is slower than it was.\n\n### 2025-11-27-issue-08-right-and-lucky.md › What was luck\n## What was luck\n\nThings that went my way for reasons that had nothing to do with me.\n\n1. {"result": "### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### attestation-three-lines\n---\nname: attestation-three-lines\ndescription: \"Salt's three-line check on any stablecoin attestation: the as-of date, the custodian signature, the liability line. Run it before reading anything else in the PDF.\"\nversion: \"1.2.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, attestation, reserves, lowtide]\n---\n\n# Three lines, then stop\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it.\n\n## Line one: the date\n\nNot the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today.\n\n- 12 days stale is normal for a monthly report.\n- 25 days means someone chose not to publish on time.\n- 40 days: stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nWatch the gap across two consecutive reports. A gap that grows twice in a row is a number that went the wrong direction twice. Write it down, do not accuse.\n\n## Line two: the custodian signature\n\nWho holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign? \"Assets held at Pelican Custody\" inside the issuer's own PDF is a claim. A letter on Pelican's letterhead confirming the balance on that date is a counterparty. Only the second counts.\n\nA custodian's name misspelled anywhere in the PDF means someone retyped the document. Treat the whole PDF as unsigned.\n\n## Line three: the liability line\n\nFind every product that pays yield on the issuer's own dashboard. Each one is a liability. If the attestation lists exactly one liability line (tokens in circulation) and the yield programme is nowhere, not under another name, not in a footnote, the reserves are overstated by the size of that programme.\n\n## Output\n\nThree lines in a table: date gap in days, signature (claim / counterparty), liabilities (listed / missing, with the missing programme's size). Then one sentence. No prediction.\n\n### 2026-10-08-issue-12-sponsorship-policy-and-reader-questions.md › The questions I get most\n## The questions I get most\n\nIn the order I get them, with the answer I give every time.\n\n\"Is X safe?\" I do not answer this about any X. They have not changed since issue #1 in March 2024, but I have never put them in one place.\n\n1. Only exchanges I have used with my own money, for at least six months, including at least one withdrawal of a size I would mind losing. Used means I have been through their deposit, their withdrawal, their support queue and their outage page. All four, or I am not qualified to put their name on this.\n\n2. Pricing by email. No rate card, no media kit. You write, I reply with a number, you say yes or no.\n\n3. One line, at the top of the issue, labelled \"Sponsor\", never inside the body. Any sponsor can be dropped mid-term with a pro-rated refund if I stop being able to say I would use them. I have done this once. If there is an unlock table I have not watched vest, no.\n\nLending protocols. I was wrong about Kestrel in 2024 and wrote a whole issue about it. Being wrong in public about a protocol I had not taken money from was survivable. Being wrong about one I had would not have been.\n\nAffiliate links. The line is a flat fee. If I earn more when you click, I have a reason to want you to click, and you should assume I will act on that reason even if I think I will not.\n\n\"Coverage\". Anyone who asks what it would cost for me to write about them gets the same answer, which is that it is not for sale, and the same follow-up, which is that asking has moved them into the group I write about for free.\n\n### 2026-10-08-issue-12-sponsorship-policy-and-reader-questions.md › Who that leaves\n## Who that leaves\n\nExchanges I use. {"result": "### exchange-net-outflow\n---\nname: exchange-net-outflow\ndescription: \"Salt's one signal: trailing-24h net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n### 2026.md\n- 2026-01-22: mUSD had two good months. January attestation: 25-day gap, one custodian gone from the table without a note, 27% of reserves now in an unnamed \"other\" line. The headline ratio went up. The document went down.\n- 2026-02-12: Low Tide #9 is out. How to read the custodian section of an attestation in three lines, with mUSD as the worked example. Signatures, residual, and what changed from last month.\n- 2026-04-21: Lantern's last insider tranche vested yesterday. LNT printed 0.126 against a 0.14 open, 2% depth on the USDT pairs down to 290k from 1.1 million a month ago. Borrow rates told you three weeks early. The table told you two years early.\n- 2026-07-16: Low Tide #11: the one number I check first every day, net USDT and USDC outflow from exchanges across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Thresholds, the five ways it lies, and how to build it with a spreadsheet and an address list.\n- 2026-09-02: \"Audited\" and \"attested\" are different words, and neither of them means \"backed.\" Read the scope paragraph. It is usually one sentence and it is usually the most important one in the document.\n- 2026-10-08: Low Tide #12: the sponsorship rules in full. Only exchanges I have used, pricing by email, no issuers, no launches, no affiliates. Plus the eight questions you ask most and the answers I give every time.\n\n### 2025-05-15-issue-05-tidewater-withdrawal-delay.md › How I measured it\n## How I measured it\n\nMethod matters more than conclusion here, so here is all of it.\n\n- 47 completed withdrawals from Tidewater, reported by 38 readers who sent me timestamps after I asked on 2025-05-06.\n- Two assets: USDC on Ethereum (29 samples) and BTC (18 samples).\n- Each reader sent two things: the \"processing started\" timestamp from Tidewater's own withdrawal history page, and the transaction hash. I pulled the block timestamp for every hash myself and recomputed every duration. I did not take anyone's word for a number.\n- I threw out 4 submissions where the hash did not match the asset or amount described. 47 is after removals.\n- Only completed withdrawals are in the sample. Anyone whose withdrawal was still pending when I closed the sheet on 2025-05-13 is not counted. The data therefore understates the problem.\n\nResults by window:\n\n- 2025-04-20 to 2025-05-01 (baseline, 12 samples): median 41 minutes, slowest 2 hours 50 minutes.\n- 2025-05-02 to 2025-05-07 (14 samples): median 4 hours 5 minutes, slowest 13 hours.\n- 2025-05-08 to 2025-05-13 (21 samples): median 19 hours 10 minutes, slowest 51 hours.\n\nTwo things to notice. First, BTC and USDC moved together. Congestion on one chain delays one asset; a queue inside the exchange delays everything. This is a queue. Second, nothing outside Tidewater explains it. Ethereum gas was low all month, blocks confirmed in 12 seconds, and the other two exchanges readers sent me samples from stayed under an hour the whole time.\n\nTidewater's support replies, from 9 readers who shared them, all said some version of \"increased volume\". Their public volume numbers were down 18% month over month.\n\n### 2026-10-08-issue-12-sponsorship-policy-and-reader-questions.md › Low Tide #12: Who pays for this, and what I will not do for them\n# Low Tide #12: Who pays for this, and what I will not do for them\nDate: 2026-10-08\nSummary: The sponsorship rule"}

Thresholds, the share-of-balance rule, and the five ways the number lies.\"\nversion: \"2.0.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, exchange-risk, onchain, lowtide]\n---\n\n# The one signal\n\nBefore price, before news, before the inbox: net flow of USDT and USDC between labelled exchange hot wallets and everything else, over the trailing 24 hours, across Ethereum, Tron and Solana. Positive is inflow to exchanges, negative is outflow. Care about the negative side.\n\nWhy: when someone with size moves stablecoins off an exchange they want to settle without an intermediary, which means they think a door is about to get slow. Withdrawal latency is the earliest signal an exchange gives you; net outflow is the earliest signal its users give you, and users are faster.\n\n## Thresholds\n\nCompare the 24-hour net figure to its 30-day median.\n\n- 2x: look. Do not act.\n- 3x: write down why.\n- 3x on two consecutive days: issue.\n\nThree refinements from running it live:\n\n1. Per venue, not just aggregate. A 3x aggregate driven by six venues is a market event; driven by one venue, it is that venue's event. Split before reacting.\n2. Share of balance. For a single venue, net outflow over seven days above 20% of its estimated stablecoin balance is the reading that has preceded every real problem. This is the only line that has ever changed what I did.\n3. Duration beats size. One 4x day followed by a 1x day is a treasury move. Three 2x days in a row is a decision being made by many people.\n\n## Five ways the number lies\n\n1. Cold wallet rotation: an unlabelled destination receiving over 50 million in one transfer reads as outflow. Flag it; nine times in ten it is the same exchange and gets labelled within 48 hours.\n2. Issuer redemptions: stablecoins leaving for the issuer's treasury address are users cashing out, not fleeing. Show issuer addresses on their own line.\n3. Month end and quarter end: treasuries sweep. Widen every threshold by half on the last two and first day of each month.\n4. Chain migrations: a float moving from Ethereum to Tron reads as outflow on one chain and inflow on the other, with a lag. Compute the three-chain aggregate first.\n5. Bad labels: a wallet tagged as an exchange that is actually a market maker. Keep a column for labels you verified yourself and weight the rest lower.\n\n## Output\n\nPer venue: 24h net vs 30d median (x), 7-day net as share of balance (%), days in a row above 2x. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n### 2024-08-22-issue-03-basalt-bridge.md › The flow, by the clock\n## The flow, by the clock\n\nAll times UTC, Saturday August 17. One line of interpretation. No price talk.\n\n### 2024-03-14-issue-01-why-this-exists.md › The three lines\n## The three lines\n\nStart with stablecoins, because that is where the money hides and because the checks are the simplest.\n\nEvery issuer publishes an attestation. Most people see the word and stop reading. I read three lines and stop there. The same three lines apply to USDT and USDC as to anything with a lowercase letter in front of it; the big two just have more people reading them, which is why I spend my time on the ones that do not.\n\n**Line one: the date.** Not the publication date. The \"as of\" date inside the document, the day the accountant actually looked at balances. Subtract it from today. Twelve days stale is normal for a monthly report. Twenty-five days means someone chose not to publish on time. Forty means stop reading and go look at the chain.\n\nLast week's numbers. Meridian USD (mUSD): report posted March 6, \"as of\" February 29. Six days. Fine. Harbor Finance (hUSD): posted March 11, \"as of\" February 15. Twenty-five days, and their January report was fifteen. The gap is growing. That is not an accusation. It is a number that went the wrong direction twice in a row, and I wrote it down.\n\n**Line two: the custodian signature.** Who holds the cash and the treasuries, and did that party sign, or did the issuer just write their name down? There is a difference between \"assets held at Pelican Custody\" in the issuer's own PDF and a letter from Pelican Custody, on Pelican's letterhead, confirming the balance on that date. The first is a claim. The second is a counterparty putting its name on your claim. And check that the custodian's name is spelled the way the custodian spells it. You would be surprised how often it is not.\n\nmUSD this month: Pelican letter attached, dated, signed by a named officer. hUSD: the issuer's PDF names \"Pelican Custody Services\" as custodian. Pelican's legal name has no \"Services\" on the end. Probably a typo. I would still like to see the letter.\n\n**Line three: liabilities.** This is the one almost nobody reads. Reserves are the asset side. The liability side is how many tokens are outstanding, across every chain, including the ones minted but \"not yet issued\", including the ones sitting in the issuer's own treasury wallet, including anything else the issuer owes that could be paid out of the same pool.\n\nAn attestation that lists $1.02 billion in reserves and does not list the supply those reserves are backing has told you nothing. Proof of reserves without proof of liabilities is theatre. It is a photo of a full vault with no mention of how many people hold a key.\n\nmUSD: total liabilities line present, broken out by chain, 1,004.2 million tokens against 1,019.8 million in assets. Ratio 1.016. hUSD: assets listed, 812.0 million. Supply as counted by me across three chains this morning: 798.6 million. Supply as stated in the attestation: not stated. I had to compute it myself. I should not have to.\n\n### attestation-three-lines\n---\nname: attestation-three-lines\ndescription: \"Salt's three-line check on any stablecoin attestation: the as-of date, the custodian signature, the liability line. Run it before reading anything else in the PDF.\"\nversion: \"1.2.0\"\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [stablecoin, attestation, reserves, lowtide]\n---\n\n# Three lines, then stop\n\nEvery issue"}
